Manin’s conjecture for integral points on toric varieties
Tim Santens

Abstract
. We formulate a conjecture on the number of integral points of bounded height on log Fano varieties in analogy with Manin’s conjecture on the number of rational points of bounded height on Fano varieties. We also give a prediction for the leading constant which is similar to Peyre’s interpretation of the leading constant in Manin’s conjecture. We give evidence for our conjecture by proving it for toric varieties. The proof is based on harmonic analysis on universal torsors.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
11D45 (Primary) 11G35, 11G50, 14G05 (Secondary)

1.
Introduction

Let
F
be a number field with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Let
X
be a geometrically integral smooth proper variety over
F
. It is expected that if
X
is
Fano
, i.e. has ample anticanonical divisor
-K_{X}
, then the set of rational points
X(F)
is either empty or large, both in a qualititative and a quantitative sense.

In this paper we will focus on the quantitative properties of
X(F)
. To do this one fixes a
height function

H:X(F)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and studies the number of rational points of bounded height
\#\{P\in X(F):H(P)\leq T\}
as
T\to\infty
. If
X
is a Fano variety then the most natural height is the one corresponding to the anticanonical divisor. Manin
[
FMT89
]
conjectured a precise asymptotic for this counting problem if
H
is an anticanonical height. Batyrev and Manin
[
BM90
]
later extended this conjecture to general height functions. A conjectural interpretation of the leading constant has been proposed by Peyre
[
Pey95
]
.

Batyrev and Tschinkel
[
BT95
,
BT98a
,
BT96
]
have studied the number of rational points on toric varieties of bounded height using harmonic analysis on the group of adelic points of the torus and have proven that Manin’s conjecture holds in this case. Chambert-Loir and Tschinkel
[
CLT10b
]
attempted to extend Batyrev and Tschinkel’s methods to count integral points on toric varieties, their argument unfortunately has certain issues. Indeed, their main theorem is false, a counterexample was recently given by Wilsch
[
Wil22
]
.

The goal of this paper is to prove a correct asymptotic for the number of integral points on toric varieties. Our main theorem is the following

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
X
be a smooth proper toric variety over
F
with open subtorus
T\subset X
. Let
D\subset X\setminus T
be a divisor and let
U:=X\setminus D
. Assume that
\overline{F}[U]^{\times}=\overline{F}^{\times}
and that the log anticanonical divisor
-K_{(X,D)}=-K_{X}+D
is big. Let
H:X(F)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be a height corresponding to a big divisor.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an integral model of
U
over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
such that
T(F)\cap\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\neq\emptyset
. There exists
b\in\mathbb{N}
and
a,c>0
such that as
T\to\infty
we have

\#\{P\in T(F)\cap\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{K}):H(P)\leq T\}\sim cT^{a}(\log T)^{b}.

Moreover, we give a geometric interpretation of
a,b
and
c
in the style of Manin’s conjecture. Let us note that this is essentially Chambert-Loir and Tschinkel’s result, except that the logarithmic exponent
b
is different.

Based on this result we make Conjecture
6.1
, a conjecture on the asymptotics of the number of points of bounded height on log Fano varieties analogous to Manin’s conjecture.

1.1.
Manin’s conjecture

Let
X
be a smooth projective Fano variety and
H:X(F)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
an anticanonical height. The modern form of Manin’s conjecture states that there exists
c>0
and a thin subset
Z\subset X(F)
, i.e.
Z
is contained in a finite union of images of
Y(F)\to X(F)
, where
Y
is integral and
Y\to X
is either a closed immersion or a finite map of degree
\geq 2
, such that

\#\{P\in X(F)\setminus Z:H(P)\leq T\}\sim cT(\log T)^{b-1}

as
T\to\infty
. Here
b:=\text{rk}\Pic X
is the rank of the Picard group
\Pic X
.

Peyre’s conjectural interpretation
[
Pey95
]
of the leading constant
c
is as follows:
c=\frac{\theta}{(b-1)!}\cdot\beta\cdot\tau(X(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{\Br})
where
\theta
is
Peyre’s effective cone constant
, a combinatorial invariant depending on the
pseudo-effective cone

\Eff_{X}\subset(\Pic X)_{\mathbb{R}}
, the closure of the cone generated by the effective divisors,
\beta=\#\HH^{1}(F,\Pic X_{\overline{F}})=\#\Br_{1}X/\Br F
is a cohomological invariant,
\tau
is the
Tamagawa measure
on the space of adelic points
X(\mathbb{A}_{F})
, this measure depends on the chosen height function
H
, and
X(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{\Br}\subset X(\mathbb{A}_{F})
is the Brauer-Manin set.

Manin’s conjecture is now known in a variety of different cases, although even the case of smooth Fano surfaces still remains far out of reach, see
[
Bro07
]
for an overview. Batyrev and Tschinkel
[
BT98a
]
have resolved the conjecture for toric varieties. We remark that one can use their method to deduce that the rational points
X(F)
are dense in
X(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{\Br}

[
CLT10b
, Cor. 3.10.4]
.

1.2.
Integral points

In this paper we will study the closely related problem of counting
integral points
on varieties. For this one fixes a boundary divisor
D\subset X
which we will always assume to have geometrically simple normal crossings, i.e. the base change to the algebraic closure
D_{\overline{F}}\subset X_{\overline{F}}
has simple normal crossings. One then expects that for any integral model
\mathcal{U}
of
U:=X\setminus D
the set of integral points
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
is either empty or large as long as the pair
(X,D)
is
log Fano
,i.e. that the log anticanonical divisor
-K_{(X,D)}:=-K_{X}-D
is an ample divisor on
X
.

The number of integral points of bounded height has been studied in a large number of different situations, some of the more recent results are
[
CLT12
,
TBT13
,
Cho19
,
Wil22
,
DW22
,
Wil23
]
, but a general Manin-type conjecture has so far remained elusive. In this paper we will make such a conjecture, let us first discuss some of the phenomena which have to be explained by such a conjecture.

Integral points are concentrated near the boundary divisor in the analytic topology at the archimedean places, as can already be seen in the simplest case where
(X,D)=(\mathbb{P}^{1},\infty)
. Moreover, it turns out that they are concentrated near the strata of maximal codimension of
D
. The appropriate Tamagawa measures thus have to be supported on these strata at the archimedean places as in
[
CLT10a
, §2.1.9]
.

The collection of strata has a combinatorial structure, following Chambert-Loir and Tschinkel
[
CLT10a
, §3.1]
we encode this structure via the
analytic Clemens complex

\mathcal{C}^{\an}_{\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}(D)
. A
face

\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{C}^{\an}_{\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}(D)
of the Clemens complex essentially consist of a choice of stratum
Z_{v}\subset D_{F_{v}}
for each archimedean place
v
. Let
Z_{v}^{\circ}
be the complement in
Z_{v}
of all the proper substrata. The
dimension

\dim(\mathbf{A})
of a face
\mathbf{A}
is a number which is closely related to the codimension of the strata
Z_{v}
, see §3.1 for the precise definitions.

Wilsch’s counterexample
[
Wil22
]
to Chambert-Loir and Tschinkel’s main result
[
CLT10b
]
is due to a so-called
analytic obstruction
to the Zariski density of integral points near certain strata. Wilsch only discusses such analytic obstructions in the split case, but a minor modification of his arguments shows that these obstructions also exist in the non-split case, see Theorem
3.13
.

For general log Fano varieties these analytic obstructions are still insufficient to explain all pathological behavior, see §
6.3.2
for an example. In that case the issue is due to the existence of non-zero global invertible sections on
U
. It is for this reason that we will assume that
\overline{F}[U]^{\times}=\overline{F}^{\times}
, implicit in our conjecture will be that under these assumptions the analytic obstructions explain all pathological behavior.

Our solution to dealing with these analytic obstructions is as follows. Because integral points tend to be concentrated around faces of the Clemens complex we will study the distribution of integral points of bounded height near each face separately. We will do this as follows: for each archimedean place
v
let
U_{Z_{v}}\subset X_{F_{v}}
be the minimal open subvariety of
X_{F_{v}}
containing
U
and
Z_{v}^{\circ}
. We can then count integral points near
\mathbf{A}
by weighting by a continuous compactly supported function
f_{v}:U_{Z_{v}}(F_{v})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. One can always reduce counting all integral points to this case by a partition of unity argument. We will then only count integral points near those faces where there is no analytic obstruction, since the integral points are not Zariski dense otherwise.

1.3.
Main results

We will deduce Theorem
1.1
from a more precise result which gives an asymptotic formula for the number of integral points near each face separately. We state this theorem only for the anticanonical height now, for more general heights the interpretation of the logarithmic power and the leading constant is more complicated.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth proper toric variety over
F
with open subtorus
T\subset X
. Let
D\subset X\setminus T
be a toric divisor and let
U:=X\setminus D
. Assume that
\overline{F}[U]^{\times}=\overline{F}^{\times}
. Assume moreover that
-K_{(X,D)}\in\Pic X
is a big divisor and let
H:X(F)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be a log anticanonical height.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{C}^{\an}_{\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}(D)
be a face of the analytic Clemens complex and assume that there is no analytic obstruction to Zariski density of integral points near
\mathbf{A}
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an integral model of
U
and assume that
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\cap T(F)\neq\emptyset
. There exists a non-empty closed subset
(\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\emph{fin}}}\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\times\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}Z_{v}^{\circ}(F_{v}))^{\Br}\subset\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\emph{fin}}}\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\times\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}Z_{v}^{\circ}(F_{v})
with the following property.

For each archimedean place
v
let
f_{v}:U_{Z_{v}}(F_{v})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a continuous function with compact support
\emph{supp}(f_{v})
such that

\Big(\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\emph{fin}}}\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\times\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}Z_{v}^{\circ}(F_{v})\Big)^{\Br}\cap\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\emph{fin}}}\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\times\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}\emph{supp}(f_{v})\neq\emptyset.

There exists a
c>0
such that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\cap T(F)\\
H(P)\leq T\end{subarray}}\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}f_{v}(P)\sim cT(\log T)^{\emph{rk}\Pic U+\dim\mathbf{A}}.

Moreover, the constant
c
has a Peyre-type interpretation.

One recovers Theorem
1.1
by letting
b
be the maximum of
\emph{rk}\Pic U+\dim\mathbf{A}
as
\mathbf{A}
ranges over all faces which have no analytic obstruction.

As the notation suggest
\big(\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\text{fin}}}\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\times\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}Z_{v}^{\circ}(F_{v})\big)^{\Br}
is a Brauer-Manin set. The relevant Brauer group
\Br(X;\mathbf{A})
is a subgroup of
\Br U
whose index is a power of
2
. It will turn out that it is enough to work with the algebraic Brauer group
\Br_{1}(X;\mathbf{A})=\Br(X;\mathbf{A})\cap\Br_{1}U
. The group
\Br(X;\mathbf{A})
depends on the face
\mathbf{A}
. This is another reason why it is crucial to work locally around each face separately.

Let us remark that the Peyre-type interpretation of the constant
c
is rather subtle. In general the Brauer group
\Br(X;\mathbf{A})/\Br F
can be infinite, in which case the Tamagawa measure of
(\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\text{fin}}}\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\times\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}Z_{v}(F_{v}))^{\Br}
is
0
. But in this case the analogue of
\beta=\#\HH^{1}(X,\Pic X)
will be infinite. It follows that the naive generalization of Peyre’s constant is
\infty\cdot 0
. See §
6.3.3
for an example.

We will resolve this as follows. By work of Salberger
[
Sal98
, Assertion 5.25]
Peyre’s constant for
X
is equal to a sum of adelic volumes on certain auxiliary varieties, the
universal torsors
of
X
. This definition of Peyre’s constant generalizes well even in the case that
\Br(X;\mathbf{A})
is infinite, at least if the algebraic Brauer-Manin obstruction is the only obstruction to strong approximation.

It turns out that one can still relate our generalization of Salberger’s formula for Peyre’s constant to the Tamagawa measure on
U
. It is completely analogous to Peyre’s constant if
\Br(X;\mathbf{A})/\Br F
is finite, otherwise it is more subtle.

The fact that the
f_{v}
and
\mathcal{U}
can be freely chosen in Theorem
1.2
implies the following approximation result.

Theorem 1.3
.

In the same situation as in Theorem
1.2
one has that the closure of
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
in
\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\emph{fin}}}\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\times\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}U_{Z_{v}}(F_{v})
contains
(\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\emph{fin}}}\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\times\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}Z_{v}^{\circ}(F_{v}))^{\Br_{1}}
.

We say that the algebraic Brauer-Manin obstruction is the only obstruction to strong approximation
(X;\mathbf{A})
.

We can compare this with a theorem of Cao and Xu
[
CX18
]
which states that the algebraic Brauer-Manin obstruction is the only obstruction to strong approximation off the infinite places for all smooth toric varieties. The assumptions in Theorem
1.3
are stronger, but the conclusion is stronger since we are also able to approximate at the infinite places. For example, if
\HH^{0}(U,\mathcal{O}_{U})=F
then we may take
\mathbf{A}=\emptyset
in which case the above theorem implies that
U(F)
is dense in
U(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{\Br}
.

1.4.
Methods

We will combine two methods which are both often used separately to count points on varieties. The first is the universal torsor method and the second is harmonic analysis. Let us be go into some more detail.

Universal torsors of a variety
X
are a notion introduced by Colliot-Thélène and Sansuc
[
CTS87
]
to study the Brauer-Manin obstruction. It is a torsor
Y\to X
under the Néron-Severi torus
T_{\NS}
, the torus whose group of characters is
\Pic X
, satisfying a certain property. A single variety
X
has multiple universal torsors, but they are all twists of each other, i.e. they become isomorphic after base change to the separable closure of
F
. The geometry and arithmetic of
Y
is simpler than the geometry and arithmetic of
X
.

In groundbreaking work
[
Sal98
]
Salberger realized that universal torsors form a natural place to study Manin’s conjecture. Rational points
P\in X(F)
are in bijection with
T_{\NS}(F)
-orbits of rational points
Q\in Y(F)
for all universal torsors
Y\to X
, but it is generally easier to count points on
Y
. Moreover, the entire Manin conjecture, including Peyre’s rather mysterious constant, is equivalent to the statement that the number of
T_{\NS}(F)
orbits on the universal torsor in height boxes is asymptotic to the volume of these height boxes.

In most applications of the universal torsor method one works with explicit integral models
\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
of the universal torsors, in which case
\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
is in bijection with
T_{\NS}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
-orbits of integral points
\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
for all possible universal torsors
\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
. This becomes especially nice when
F=\mathbb{Q}
or a complex quadratic extensions of
\mathbb{Q}
and
T_{\NS}
is split since in this case
T_{\NS}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
is finite. In more general situations one has to fix a fundamental domain of the action of the finitely generated group
T_{\NS}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
which is usually rather technical and notationally cumbersome. We will avoid fixing a fundamental domain by counting orbits directly.

Universal torsors of toric varieties are particularly simple, they are just the complement of certain explicit codimension
2
subvarieties in affine space. Proving Manin’s conjecture in this way reduces to a lattice point counting problem. In the case when
F=\mathbb{Q}
this problem was solved by Salberger
[
Sal98
, §11]
. Universal torsors have also proven useful when counting integral points on varieties, see
[
Wil22
,
DW22
,
Wil23
]
.

A significant part of this paper is spent generalizing Salberger’s theory. As a first step one notices that Salberger’s theory does not generalize if one just uses the universal torsor of
U
. Indeed, an important ingredient of Salberger’s theory is that the order of growth in Manin’s conjecture is equal to the order of growth of certain increasing volumes on
T_{\NS}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
. This is false when one studies integral points.

A crucial observation is that the above statement becomes true if one enlarges the adelic space
T_{\NS}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
at the archimedean places in a way which depends on the face
\mathbf{A}
. We will encode this by replacing
T_{\NS}
by an
adelic group of multiplicative type

\mathbf{T}_{\NS}
, this is essentially a choice of a group of multiplicative type
T_{v}
over
F_{v}
for each place
v
, such that for all but finitely many places
T_{v}
is the base change of a group of multiplicative type
T_{F}
over
F
.

To generalize Salberger’s theory we need to replace
Y\to X
by a
\mathbf{T}_{\NS}
-torsor. To do this we will need to replace
X
and
Y
by an
adelic variety

(X;\mathbf{A})
and its
adelic universal torsor

\mathbf{Y}
respectively. An adelic variety consists of a variety
X_{v}
over
F_{v}
for each place
v
, which is the base change of a variety
X_{F}
over
F
for all but finitely many
v
. The adelic variety
(X;\mathbf{A})
will depend on the choice of a face
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{C}^{\an}_{\Omega_{F}^{\infty}}
. The invariants of
X
which play a role in Manin’s conjecture, such as the pseudo-effective cone and the Brauer group, will be replaced by the analogous invariants of the adelic variety
(X;\mathbf{A})
. The group
\Pic(X;\mathbf{A})
has been defined before by Wilsch
[
Wil22
, Def. 2.2.1]
in the split case. The definition of
\Br(X;\mathbf{A})
is completely new. The above notions are precisely defined in §3.

An extra difficulty which appears when counting integral points is that the group of multiplicative type
T_{\NS}
is not necessarily connected. Tamagawa measures of tori play an important role in Salberger’s work. We were unable to find a definition of such Tamagawa measures in the literature for non-connected algebraic groups, so we generalize this ourselves.

Another obstruction to stating a general Manin-type conjecture for integral points is that the relevant Brauer group modulo constants can be infinite. This implies that Peyre’s interpretation of the leading constant is
\infty\cdot 0
. But Salberger’s formula for Peyre’s constant as a sum of adelic volumes over each universal torsor still works. It is not immediately clear how to relate this to a Tamagawa volume on
(X;\mathbf{A})
. Our solution will be compute Peyre’s constant for finite subgroups of
\Br_{1}(X;\mathbf{A})
and then take the limit of these constants over all finite subgroups.

To relate this limit to adelic volumes on the universal torsor we generalize Colliot-Thélène and Sansuc’s theory of descent
[
CTS87
]
to adelic varieties to show that universal torsors and the algebraic Brauer-Manin pairing are dual in §6. The equality of the two versions of Peyre’s constant then follows from an application of Poisson summation to certain adelic cohomology groups.

A theoretical innovation which is not strictly required for our method but which simplifies certain arguments will be the notion of
metrics
and their corresponding
heights
on torsors. This generalizes the usual theory of metrics and heights on line bundles, via the equivalence between line bundles and
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-torsors. We study these notions in §5. A metric on a universal torsor is essentially a compatible choice of metrics on all line bundles simultaneously. There exists a related notion in the literature, that of a system of metrics and their lifts to universal torsors
[
Pey98
, §4]
, but the notion of a metric on a torsor seems to be more natural.

We can then state our integral version of Manin’s conjecture in §7, including our prediction for the leading constant, both in terms of volumes on universal torsors and in terms of volumes of Brauer-Manin sets.

After these theoretical preparations we start counting points on toric varieties in §7. We first reduce our counting problem to counting
T_{\NS}(F)
-orbits of points on the universal torsors of
U
. These universal torsors naturally have the structure of toric varieties, whose underlying torus is a Weil restriction torus
\Theta
. Moreover, the action of
T_{\NS}
factors through the action of
\Theta
so it suffice to count elements of the group
\Theta(F)/T_{\NS}(F)
. Using a Tauberian theorem it suffices to show that the
height zeta function

Z(s,f)=\sum_{P\in\Theta(F)/T_{\NS}(F)}f(P)H(P)^{-s}
has an appropriate meromorphic continuation.

Similarly the universal torsors of the adelic variety
(X;\mathbf{A})
naturally have the structure of an adelic toric variety whose adelic torus
\Theta_{\mathbf{A}}
is a Weil restriction torus. The group
\Theta(F)/T_{\NS}(F)
embeds as a discrete group into
\Theta_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/T_{\NS}(F)
.

We then compute the Fourier transform of a function
\Theta_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/T_{\NS}(F)\to\mathbb{C}
extending
f(P)H(P)^{-s}
, for this we use the computational tools developed in
[
CLT10a
]
. We then apply Poisson summation to show that
Z(s,f)
is equal to an integral over the Pontryagin dual
(\Theta_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/\Theta(F))^{\vee}
of this Fourier transform. This Pontryagin dual is an extension of a discrete group by a real vector space. But this vector space has a canonical basis since
\Theta_{\mathbf{A}}
is a Weil restriction torus. The integral over this vector space is thus an iterated contour integral which we can compute with the residue theorem.

We remark that Chambert-Loir and Tschinkel similarly use harmonic analysis in
[
CLT10b
]
, but they do this on
T(\mathbb{A}_{F})
where
T\subset X
is the dense open torus. The main technical obstacle is then that the Pontryagin dual
(T(\mathbb{A}_{F})/T(F))^{\vee}
is an extension of a discrete group by a vector space without a canonical basis. To deal with the integral over this vector space they have to use a technical theorem
[
BT98a
, Thm. 6.17]
of Batyrev and Tschinkel. It is in the application of this theorem where the error is made. The existence of the canonical basis is the main reason why we prefer to work with the universal torsor.

It remains to determine the leading constant given by the characters contributing to the pole of highest order in, this is carried out in §8. If
H
is an anticanonical height then there is only a single character contributing to the main pole and the leading constant is seen to be the volume of an adelic subset of the universal torsor. For general heights, we apply Poisson summation to the sum over the contributing characters to identify the leading constant.

We remark that if one specializes this method to the case where
D=\emptyset
one gets a new proof of Manin’s conjecture for toric varieties. This proof does not use the technical theorem
[
BT98a
, Thm. 6.17]
, replacing it by the residue theorem. The cost for this analytic simplification is that the geometric set-up is more involved.

To conclude the introduction let us mention that the crucial geometric reason why our method works is that universal torsors of toric varieties are themselves (very simple) toric varieties and that the action of
T_{\NS}
factors through the toric action. The only varieties with this property are toric varieties, but there are other geometric objects with this property, namely
toric stacks
. The methods in this paper actually originated from the author attempting to understand integral and rational points of bounded height on toric stacks. In future work the author will extend the methods to count points on toric stacks.

1.5.
Conventions and notations

For every field
F
we fix a separable closure
F^{\sep}
and an algebraic closure
\overline{F}
. We let
\Gamma_{F}:=\Gal(F^{\sep}/F)
be its absolute Galois group.

If
A
is a
\Gamma_{F}
-set then we let
F[A]
be the corresponding étale algebra.

If
B\to A
is a map and
N
is an object living over
A
then we will use the notation
N_{B}
for the base change of
N
to
B
. The precise meaning will depend on the context, e.g. if
\mathcal{L}
is a line bundle on a scheme
A
then
\mathcal{L}_{B}
the pullback along
B\to A
.

All cohomology/fundamental groups are étale cohomology/fundamental groups. We will omit the base point since it is never relevant. If
G
is a smooth group scheme over a scheme
X
then
H^{1}(X,G)
classifies
G
-torsors over
X
. We will freely use this identification.

If
U\subset X
is a subset then we denote by
\mathbf{1}_{U}:X\to\mathbb{C}
the indicator function of
U
.

If
V
is a finite-dimensional vector space over a field
F
and
V^{*}:=\Hom(V,F)
is the dual vector space then we will denote the perfect pairing
V\times V^{*}\to F
by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{V}
.

By a
measure
we always mean a Radon measure, i.e. a functional on the space of compactly supported functions.

Let
S^{1}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|=1\}
be the complex unit circle, considered as a locally compact abelian group. For a locally compact abelian group
G
we let
G^{\vee}:=\Hom(G,S^{1})
be the Pontryagin dual.

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space and
\mu
a measure on
X
. If a discrete group
G
acts on
X
and the action is free and proper then the quotient
X/G
is also locally compact Hausdorff. If the action preserves
d\mu
then we will abuse notation to denote the quotient measure on
X/G
also by
\mu
. This means that for every compactly supported continuous function
f:X\to\mathbb{C}
we have
\int_{X}f(x)d\mu(x)=\int_{X/G}\sum_{y\in xG}f(y)d\mu(xG)
.

2.
Preliminaries

Let us start with the following simple fact, which we will frequently use.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
X
be a variety over a field
F
,
D\subset X
a divisor and
A
its set of irreducible components. Let
B\subset A
be a subset and
U=X\setminus(\cup_{D_{a}\in A\setminus B}D_{a})
. If
F[U]^{\times}=F^{\times}
then the following sequence of groups is exact

0\to\mathbb{Z}[A]\to\Pic X\times\mathbb{Z}[B]\to\Pic U\to 0

Where the first map is the product of
\mathbb{Z}[A]\to\Pic X:D_{a}\to[D_{a}]
and the projection
\mathbb{Z}[A]\to\mathbb{Z}[B]
and the second map is the sum of the restriction
\Pic X\to\Pic U
and the map
\mathbb{Z}[B]\to\Pic U:D_{b}\to[D_{b}]
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from the standard exact sequence

0\to\mathbb{Z}[A\setminus B]\to\Pic X\to\Pic U\to 0

∎

2.1.
Number fields

Let
F
be number field or non-archimedean local field. We will denote its ring of integers by
\mathcal{O}_{F}
.

If
F
is a number field then we let
\Omega_{F}
be its set of places,
\Omega_{F}^{\infty}
its set of archimedean places and
\Omega_{F}^{\text{fin}}:=\Omega_{F}\setminus\Omega_{F}^{\infty}
its set of non-archimedean places. For a place
v\in\Omega_{F}
we write
F_{v}
for the completion at
v
. For each place
v
we fix an isomorphism
\overline{F}\cong\overline{F}_{v}
, inducing the decomposition subgroup
\Gamma_{v}:=\Gamma_{F_{v}}\subset\Gamma_{F}
. If
v\in\Omega_{F}^{\text{fin}}
is a non-archimedean place then we let
\mathcal{O}_{v}:=\mathcal{O}_{F_{v}}
be its ring of integers,
\pi_{v}
a uniformizer,
\mathbb{F}_{v}:=\mathcal{O}_{v}/\pi_{v}\mathcal{O}_{v}
the residue field and
q_{v}=\#\mathbb{F}_{v}
.

For any place let
dx_{v}
be the Haar measure on
F_{v}
. We normalize this in the standard way such that the induced product Haar measure
dx=\prod_{v}dx_{v}
on the adeles
\mathbb{A}_{F}
exists and
dx(\mathbb{A}_{F}/F)=1
. To be precise
dx_{v}(\mathcal{O}_{v})=\Disc(F_{v})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
where
\Disc(F_{v})
is the absolute discriminant of
F_{v}
if
v
is non-archimedean,
dx_{v}
is the Lebesgue measure if
v
is real and
dx_{v}
is
2
times the Lebesgue measure if
v
is complex. We normalize all places
v
such that
dx_{v}(a\Omega)=|a|_{v}dx_{v}(\Omega)
for all
a\in F_{v}
and measurable
\Omega\subset F_{v}
. This means that if
v
is non-archimedean then
|\pi_{v}|_{v}=q_{v}^{-1}
, if
v
is real then
|\cdot|_{v}
is the absolute value on
\mathbb{R}
and if
v
is complex then
|\cdot|_{v}
is the square of the absolute value on
\mathbb{C}
.

A
Hecke quasi-character
is a multiplicative continuous map
\chi:\mathbb{G}_{m}(\mathbb{A}_{F})\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. It is a product of local quasi-characters
\chi_{v}:\mathbb{G}_{m}(F_{v})\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
for all places
v
. Given such a local, resp. global, quasi-character we let
L_{v}(s;\chi_{v})
, resp.
L(s;\chi)
be the corresponding local, resp. global
L
-function as in Tate’s thesis. If
\chi=1
then we let
\zeta_{F}(s):=L(s;1)
be the global Zeta function.

Given a finitely generated
\Gamma_{v}
, resp.
\Gamma_{F}
, module
M
we denote by
L_{v}(s,M)
, resp.
L(s,M)
, the local, resp. global, Artin
L
-function of the Galois representation
M\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.

Let
\zeta^{*}_{F}(1)
, resp.
L^{*}(1,L)
, denote the residue of
\zeta_{F}(s)
, resp.
L(s,M)
, at
s=1
.

2.2.
Twists of torsors

The following is explained in more detail and generality in
[
Sko01
, §2.2]
. Let
S
be a scheme and
G
a commutative smooth group scheme over
S
. Every right
G
-torsor
a:T\to S
is also a left
G
-torsor
[
Sko01
, p. 13]
. The inverse torsor
a^{-1}:T\to S
has the same underlying scheme as
T
but it is a left
G
-torsor via the action
g\cdot_{a^{-1}}t=g^{-1}\cdot_{a}t
.

Let
X
be a scheme over
S
and
\pi:Y\to X
a left
G
-torsor. The
twisted torsor

[
Sko01
, p.20, Ex. 2]

\pi_{a}:Y_{a}\to X
is the left
G
-torsor given by the contracted product
T\times^{G}Y:=T\times Y/G
. The action of
G
is the diagonal one

G\times_{S}T\times_{S}Y\to T\times_{S}Y:(g;t,y)\to(t\cdot_{a^{-1}}g^{-1},g\cdot y)=(g\cdot_{a}t,g\cdot y)

The cohomology class of
\pi_{a}:Y_{a}\to X
is equal to
[Y]-a\in H^{1}(X,G)
.

We can use torsors to study points on
X
using the following lemma
[
Poo17
, Thm. 8.4.1]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

The images
\pi_{a}(Y_{a}(S))
of the twists
\pi_{a}:Y_{a}\to X
are pairwise disjoint and

X(S)=\coprod_{a\in H^{1}(S,G)}\pi_{a}(Y_{a}(S)).

Let
f:G\to G^{\prime}
be a group homomorphism of commutative group schemes over
S
and let
Y^{\prime}:=G^{\prime}\times^{G}Y
be the balanced product. Let
a:T\to S
be a left
G
-torsor. The image of
a\in\HH^{1}(S,G)
along the pushforward
f_{*}:\HH^{1}(S,G)\to\HH^{1}(S,G^{\prime})
is given by the contracted product
a^{\prime}:T^{\prime}:=G^{\prime}\times^{G}T\to S
. There is a canonical map

(1)

Y_{a}=T\times^{G}Y\to G^{\prime}\times^{G}(T\times^{G}Y)=(G^{\prime}\times^{G}T)\times^{G^{\prime}}(G^{\prime}\times^{G}Y)=T^{\prime}\times^{G^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}=Y^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}.

2.3.
Group of multiplicative type

We recall the following definition.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
B
be a scheme and
L
an étale sheaf on
B
which is étale locally a constant finitely generated group. The
Cartier dual
of
N
is the étale sheaf

D(N):=\Hom_{B}(N,\mathbb{G}_{m}).

A group scheme
T
over
B
is a
group of multiplicative type
if it isomorphic as an étale sheaf to
D(N)
for some
N
. Such a group
N
is canonically isomorphic to the sheaf of characters
X^{*}(T):=\Hom(T,\mathbb{G}_{m})
.

A group of multiplicative type
T
is a
torus
if
X^{*}(T)
is étale locally free.

A torus is
split
if it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}
for some
n
.

The functors
D(\cdot)
and
X^{*}(\cdot)
form an anti-equivalence between the category of sheaves which are étale locally finitely generated and group schemes of multiplicative type.

Let
B
be an integral normal scheme with fraction field
F
. It follows from
[
Sta18
,
Tag 0DV5
]
that the category of sheaves which are étale locally finitely generated is equivalent to the category of finitely generated
\pi_{1}(B)
-modules. In this case we will write
X^{*}(T)
for this
\pi_{1}(B)
-module and
\hat{T}
for the set of characters
T\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
, which is equal to
X^{*}(T)^{\pi_{1}(B)}
.

Let
T
be a group of multiplicative type over
F
. The above shows that there exists a group of multiplicative type
\mathcal{T}
over
B
with generic fiber
T
if and only if the
\Gamma_{F}
-action on the group of characters
X^{*}(T_{F})
factors through the quotient
\pi_{1}(B)
. The group of multiplicative type
\mathcal{T}
is unique in this case, so we will also denote it by
T
. If the group of multiplicative type exists then we say that
T
has
good reduction
over
B
. The action of
\Gamma_{F}
on
X^{*}(T)
factors through a finite quotient so there always exists an open subscheme
B^{\prime}\subset B
such that
T
has good reduction over
B^{\prime}
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
F
be a local field with norm
|\cdot|
and
T
a group of multiplicative type with group of global characters
\hat{T}
.

(1)

The
norm
is the map

|\cdot|_{T}:T(F)\to\Hom(\hat{T},\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\times}):x\to(\chi\to|\chi(x)|).

There is a map
\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}:z\otimes r\to r^{z}=\exp(z\log r)
. We will also call the following composition the norm

T(F)\xrightarrow{|\cdot|_{T}}\Hom(\hat{T},\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\times})\subset\Hom(\hat{T}\otimes\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}^{\times}).

Given
\chi\in\hat{T}_{\mathbb{C}}
we will use the notation
|\cdot|_{T}^{\chi}:=|\cdot|_{T}(\chi):T(F)\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

(2)

Let
\log:\Hom(\hat{T},\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\times})\to\Hom(\hat{T},\mathbb{R})=\hat{T}^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
be the composition with the logarithm. The map
\log|\cdot|_{T}:T(F)\to\hat{T}^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}
will be called the
log norm
.

(3)

The set of
integral elements

T(\mathcal{O}_{F})
is the kernel of the norm map.

Remark 2.5
.

If
T
has good reduction over
\mathcal{O}_{F}
and
\mathcal{T}
is its integral model then we have
T(\mathcal{O}_{F})=\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
. Indeed, after a base change we may assume that
T
is split, in which case it is obvious.

If
F
is non-archimedean and its residue field has size
q
then
\log|\cdot|_{T}
factors through the lattice
\Hom(\hat{T},(\log q)\mathbb{Z})\subset\Hom(\hat{T},\mathbb{R})
.

If
F
is archimedean then
|\cdot|_{T}
is surjective.

Example 2.6
.

The following torus will play a crucial role. Let
A
be a finite
\Gamma_{F}
-set and
F[A]
the corresponding étale algebra, i.e. every orbit
a\in A/\Gamma_{F}
defines a field extension
F_{a}
and
F[A]=\prod_{a\in A/\Gamma_{F}}F_{a}
. Let
\mathbb{Z}[A]
be the
\Gamma_{F}
-module freely generated by
A
.

Let
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{A}:=\Res_{F[A]/F}\mathbb{G}_{m}
be the Weil-restriction torus. It is the torus whose group of characters is
\mathbb{Z}[A]
.

We will frequently make use of the following well-known facts.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
F
be any field and
A
a finite
\Gamma_{F}
-set. Then
\HH^{1}(F,\mathbb{Z}[A])=\HH^{1}(F,\mathbb{G}_{m}^{A})=0
.

Proof.

By Shapiro’s lemma
\HH^{1}(F,\mathbb{Z}[A])=\HH^{1}(F[A],\mathbb{Z})=0
. By Hilbert theorem 90 we have
\HH^{1}(F,\mathbb{G}_{m}^{A})=\HH^{1}(F[A],\mathbb{G}_{m})=0
.
∎

2.4.
Toric varieties

Let us recall some facts about toric varieties over non-separably closed fields.

Let
X
be a smooth toric variety over a field
F
containing a rational point in the torus
T
, i.e.
X
is equipped with an action of
T
and a
T
-equivariant embedding
T\subset X
. Let
M:=X^{*}(T)
be the
\Gamma_{F}
-module of characters of
T
and
N:=\Hom(M,\mathbb{Z})=\Hom(\mathbb{G}_{m},T)
the
\Gamma_{F}
module of cocharacters. The toric variety
X_{F^{\sep}}
corresponds to a regular fan
\Sigma
on
N
. The action of
\Gamma_{F^{\sep}}
on
T
extends to
X_{F^{\sep}}
. This implies that the
\Gamma_{F}
action on
N
preserves the fan
\Sigma
. We call such a fan
\Gamma_{F}
-invariant

Remark 2.8
.

The datum of the
T
-equivariant embedding
T\subset X
is completely determined by the
\Gamma_{F}
-equivariant fan
\Sigma
.

There exists
\Gamma_{F}
-equivariant fans
\Sigma
which do not have a corresponding toric variety over
F

[
Hur11
, Thm. 1.31]
. See
[
Hur11
, Thm. 1.22]
for a general criterion when the toric variety exists. In this paper we will restrict attention to those fans for which a toric variety exists.

There does always exist a corresponding toric algebraic space over
F
since Galois descent is effective for algebraic spaces. Our arguments will also apply to toric algebraic spaces, we restricted ourselves to toric varieties to keep the presentation clearer.

Let
\Sigma_{\min}
be the
\Gamma_{F}
-set of rays of
\Sigma
. For each ray
\rho\in\Sigma_{\min}
let
n_{\rho}
be a generator of
\rho\cap N
. These
n_{\rho}
induce a map
\mathbb{Z}[\Sigma_{\min}]\to N
, which dually induces a map
M\to\mathbb{Z}[\Sigma_{\min}]
. This induces the following fundamental exact sequence of
\Gamma_{F}
-modules.

(2)

0\to F^{\sep}[X]^{\times}/F^{\sep,\times}\to M\to\mathbb{Z}[\Sigma_{\min}]\to\Pic X_{F^{\sep}}\to 0.

Moreover the effective cone is generated by the image of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[\Sigma_{\min}]
and

(3)

-K_{X}=\sum_{\rho\in\Sigma_{\min}}[D_{\rho}].

Construction 2.9
.

The
\Gamma_{F}
-set
\Sigma_{\min}
corresponds to an étale algebra
F[\Sigma_{\min}]
over
F
. Let
\mathbb{A}^{\Sigma_{\min}}:=\Res_{F[\Sigma_{\min}]/F}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{F[\Sigma_{\min}]}
be the Weil restriction. The Weil restriction torus
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\Sigma_{\min}}:=\Res_{F[\Sigma_{\min}]/F}\mathbb{G}_{m,F[\Sigma_{\min}]}
acts on this variety in the obvious way and also equivariantly embeds into it. This makes
\mathbb{A}^{\Sigma_{\min}}
a toric variety.

The group of characters is
X^{*}(\Sigma_{\min})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\Sigma_{\min}]
as a
\Gamma_{F}
module. The
\Gamma_{F}
-invariant fan corresponding to
\mathbb{A}^{\Sigma_{\min}}
consists of the cones

\Lambda_{\theta}:=\prod_{\rho\in\theta}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}[\rho]\subset\mathbb{R}[\Sigma_{\min}]

for all subsets
\theta\subset\Sigma_{\min}
.

Let
Y_{\Sigma}\subset\mathbb{A}^{\Sigma_{\min}}
be the open toric subvariety corresponding to the subfan
\Sigma^{\prime}
consisting of all cones
\Lambda_{\theta}
such that
\theta\subset\Sigma_{\min}
is the set of rays of a cone of
\Sigma
. It exists because Galois descent is effective for open immersions. The map
\mathbb{Z}[\Sigma_{\min}]\to N
induces a map of fans
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
. Let
\pi:Y_{\Sigma}\to X
be the induced morphism of toric varieties, it exists because Galois descent is effective for morphisms.

Let
T_{\NS}=D(\Pic X_{F^{\sep}})
be the Cartier dual. The surjective map
\mathbb{Z}[\Sigma_{\min}]\to\Pic X_{F^{\sep}}
induces an injective morphism
T_{\NS}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\Sigma_{\min}}
. Equip the toric variety
Y_{\Sigma}
with the
T_{\NS}
-action which is the inverse action to the one coming from this inclusion.

Proposition 2.10
.

Assume that
F^{\sep}[X]^{\times}=F^{\sep,\times}
. The
T_{\NS}
-action on
Y_{\Sigma}
makes
\pi:Y_{\Sigma}\to X
a
T_{\NS}
-torsor. It is a universal torsor.

We will discuss universal torsors in more detail in
\lx@sectionsign 5
.

Proof.

The case when
X
is a split proper toric variety is
[
Sal98
, Prop. 8.5]
.

The case when
X
is split but non-proper can be proven analogously. It also follows from the proper case by Lemma
5.9
.

The general case follows since both properties can be checked after base change to
F^{\sep}
.
∎

The twists of this universal torsor are all isomorphic as varieties.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
a\in\HH^{1}(F,T_{\NS})
and let
\pi_{a}:Y_{\Sigma,a}\to X
be a twist of the
T_{\NS}
-torsor
\pi:Y_{\Sigma}\to X
. Then there exists an isomorphism
Y_{\Sigma,a}\cong Y_{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

The action of
T_{\NS}
on
Y_{\Sigma}
factors through the action of
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\Sigma_{\min}}
so by functoriality of twists
Y_{\Sigma,a}
is a twist of the variety
Y_{\Sigma,a}
by a certain
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\Sigma_{\min}}
-torsor. The proposition now follows since
\HH^{1}(F,\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\Sigma_{\min}})=0
by Lemma
2.7
.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

If one carefully expands the above proof one can find the following description of the map
\pi_{a}:Y_{\Sigma}\cong Y_{\Sigma,a}\to X
.

The exact sequence
1\to T_{\NS}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\Sigma_{\min}}\to T\to 1
of groups of multiplicative type leads to a surjective map
T(F)\to\HH^{1}(F,T_{\NS})
since
\HH^{1}(F,\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\Sigma_{\min}})=0
. Choose a lift
t\in T(F)
of
a\in\HH^{1}(F,T_{\NS})
. The torus
T
acts on
X
and the map
\pi_{a}
is given as the composition
Y_{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\pi}X\xrightarrow{t^{-1}\cdot}X
. This map depends on the chosen lift
t
, different choices corresponding to different choices of an isomorphism
Y_{\Sigma}\cong Y_{\Sigma,a}

2.5.
\mathcal{X}
-functions of cones

We will recall the notion of characteristic
\mathcal{X}
-functions of cones from
[
BT98a
, §5]
and slightly extend it to allow torsion in
A
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
A
be a finitely generated abelian group with dual lattice
A^{*}:=\Hom(A,\mathbb{Z})
and torsion subgroup
A_{\text{tors}}
. The Haar measure
d_{A}\mathbf{x}
on
A_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}:=\Hom(A,\mathbb{R})
is defined by normalizing the volume of
A^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}/A^{*}
to be
(\#A_{\text{tors}})^{-1}
.

We normalize the measure this way because of the following lemma.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
0\to A\to B\to C\to 0
be an exact sequence of abelian groups. Then
0\to C^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}\to B^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}\to A^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}\to 0
is an exact sequence of
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces and
d_{B}\mathbf{x}=d_{A}\mathbf{x}d_{C}\mathbf{x}

Proof.

The exactness of
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces is clear.

By applying
\Hom(\cdot,\mathbb{Z})
we get a long exact sequence

0\to C^{*}\to B^{*}\to A^{*}\to\Ext^{1}(C,\mathbb{Z})\to\Ext^{1}(B,\mathbb{Z})\to\Ext^{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\to 0

Note that
\#\Ext^{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})=\#\Hom(A,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})=\#A_{\text{tors}}
for any finitely generated abelian group
A
. So we find that
\#\coker(B^{*}\to A^{*})=\#A_{\text{tors}}\#C_{\text{tors}}/\#B_{\text{tors}}
.

By the snake lemma we get an exact sequence

0\to C^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}/C^{*}\to B^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}/B^{*}\to A^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}/\text{im}(B^{*})\to 0.

The measures are thus compatible if
d_{B}\mathbf{x}(B^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}/B^{*})=d_{C}\mathbf{x}(C^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}/C^{*})d_{A}\mathbf{x}(A^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}/\text{im}(B^{*}))
. This is true because
d_{A}\mathbf{x}(A^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}/\text{im}(B^{*}))=\#\coker(B^{*}\to A^{*})d_{A}\mathbf{x}(A^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}/A^{*})
.
∎

Let
(A,A_{\mathbb{R}},\Lambda)
be a triple consisting of a finitely generated group,
A_{\mathbb{R}}:=A\otimes\mathbb{R}
an
\mathbb{R}
-vector space of dimension
k
and
\Lambda\subset A_{\mathbb{R}}
a convex
k
-dimensional cone such that
\Lambda\cap-\Lambda=0
. Denote by
\Lambda^{\circ}
the interior of
\Lambda
and by
\Lambda^{\circ}_{\mathbb{C}}:=\Lambda^{\circ}+iA_{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
(A^{*},A_{\mathbb{R}}^{*},\Lambda^{*})
be the triple consisting of the dual lattice
A^{*}:=\Hom(A,\mathbb{Z})
, the dual vector space
A^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}:=\Hom(A_{\mathbb{R}},\mathbb{R})
and the dual cone
\Lambda^{*}:=\{f\in A^{*}_{\mathbb{R}}:f(\lambda)\geq 0\text{ for all }\lambda\in\Lambda\}
.

Definition 2.15
.

The
\mathcal{X}
-function
of the triple
(A,A_{\mathbb{R}},\Lambda)
is defined as the integral

\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}(\bm{s}):=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}e^{-\langle\bm{s},\mathbf{y}\rangle}d\mathbf{y}.

for
\bm{s}\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{\circ}
.

The function
\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}(\bm{s})
is a rational function in
\mathbf{s}

[
BT98a
, Prop. 5.4]
and thus has a meromorphic continuation to
A_{\mathbb{C}}
. The following lemma determines the order of its poles.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
C\subset\Lambda
be a face,
C_{\mathbb{R}}\subset A_{\mathbb{R}}
the vector space generated by
C
,
C^{\circ}\subset C
the interior of
C
as a subspace of
C_{\mathbb{R}}
and
C^{\circ}_{\mathbb{C}}:=C^{\circ}+iC_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Let
\ell\in C^{\circ}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
a\in\Lambda^{\circ}
. Let
b(\ell)
be the codimension of
C
. For each
a\in\Lambda^{\circ}
the rational function
s^{b(\ell)}\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}(\ell+as)
in the complex variable
s
is holomorphic for
\mathrm{Re}(s)\geq 0
.

If
\ell=0
then
s^{\emph{rk}A}\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}(as)=\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}(a)
.

Proof.

If
\Lambda
and thus
\Lambda^{*}
are simplicial then this follows from the computation
[
BT98a
, Prop. 5.3:(ii)]
. In general we can cover
\Lambda^{*}
by simplicial cones
\Lambda_{1}^{*},\cdots,\Lambda_{n}^{*}\subset\Lambda^{*}
which are dual to cones
\Lambda\subset\Lambda_{1},\cdots,\Lambda_{n}
. We then by definition have
\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}(\bm{s})=\sum_{i}\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda_{i}}(\bm{s})
, cf. the proof of
[
BT98a
, Prop. 5.4]
.

The face
C
is contained in a face of
\Lambda_{i}
of the same or smaller codimension since
\Lambda\subset\Lambda_{i}
so we reduce to the simplicial case.

The last statement follows from
[
BT98a
, Prop. 5.3(i)]
.
∎

2.6.
Poisson summation

Let
H\subset G
be a closed inclusion of locally compact abelian groups. Let
\mu
and
\nu
be Haar measures on
G
and
H
and let
\mu^{\vee}
, resp.
\nu^{\vee}
, be the dual Haar measures on
G^{\vee}
, resp.
H^{\vee}
. Let
\mu/\nu
be the quotient measure on
G/H
and
(\mu/\nu)^{\vee}
the dual measure on
(G/H)^{\vee}=\ker(G^{\vee}\to H^{\vee})
.

Let
f:G\to\mathbb{C}
be an
L^{1}
function and

\hat{f}:\hat{G}\to\mathbb{C}:\chi\to\int_{G}f(g)\chi(g)d\mu(g)

the Fourier transform of
f
.

In this paper we will use the following version of the Poisson summation formula. Note the similarity with the condition on
f
and
[
Bou11
, Cor. 3.36]
.

Theorem 2.17
.

Let
f:G\to\mathbb{C}
be a continuous
L^{1}
function such that
\hat{f}
is
L^{1}
when restricted to
(G/H)^{\vee}
. Assume one of the following conditions.

(1)

For all
u\in U
in an open neighbourhood
U\subset G
of the identity the integral
\int_{H}f(uh)d\nu(h)
converges absolutely and is continuous in
u
at
1
.

(2)

There exists a continuous
L^{1}
function
F:G\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
whose Fourier transform
\hat{F}
is
L^{1}
when restricted to
(G/H)^{\vee}
and such that there exists open neighborhood
U\subset G
of the identity such that
|f(g)|\leq F(ug)
for all
u\in U
and
g\in G
.

Then
f
is
L^{1}
when restricted to
H
and

(4)

\int_{H}f(h)d\nu(h)=\int_{(G/H)^{\vee}}\hat{f}(\chi)d(\mu/\nu)^{\vee}(\chi).

Proof.

The first case is the usual Poisson summation formula
[
Bou11
, Thm. 3.35]
. In the second case the Poisson summation formula implies that for all
u\in G
outside of a measure
0
subset one has that
H\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}:h\to F(uh)
is an
L^{1}
function.

Since
U
is open such an
u\in U
exists. Let
V\subset U
be an open neighbourhood of
1
such that
u\cdot V\subset U
. For all
v^{-1}\in V
and
h\in H
one then has that
|f(vh)|\leq F(v^{-1}uvh)=F(uh)
since
v^{-1}u\in U
. The sum

\sum_{h\in H}f(vh)

thus converges and is continuous by the dominated convergence theorem. By the first case this implies (
4
).
∎

3.
Adelic families

Let
F
be a number field
1
1

1

Essentially everything in this section can be generalized to global fields.
. When dealing with integral points on non-proper varieties we will consider families of algebro-geometric objects over
F_{v}
for every place
v
, almost all of which come from an object over
F
. In this section we will discuss such families.

Definition 3.1
.

(1)

An
adelic scheme over
F
is a tuple
\mathbf{X}=(X_{F},(X_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(\xi_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
where
X_{F}
is a scheme over
F
,
X_{v}
is a scheme over
\mathbb{F}_{v}
and
\xi_{v}:(X_{F})_{F_{v}}\to X_{v}
is a morphism and an isomorphism for all but finitely many places
v
.

(2)

A
morphism

\mathbf{f}:\mathbf{Y}\to\mathbf{X}
of adelic schemes is a tuple
(f_{F},(f_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{v}})
where
f_{F}:X_{F}\to Y_{F}
and
f_{v}:X_{v}\to Y_{v}
are maps such that the following diagram commutes for all
v\in\Omega_{F}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{Y,v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{X,v}}

(3)

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a property of schemes over fields stable under base change. An adelic scheme
\mathbf{X}
is
\mathcal{P}
if
X_{F}
and all the
X_{v}
are
\mathcal{P}
.

(4)

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a property of morphisms of schemes over fields stable under base change. A morphism of adelic schemes
\mathbf{f}
is
\mathcal{P}
if
f_{F}
and all the
f_{v}
are
\mathcal{P}
.

Remark 3.2
.

We will use bold letters to denote adelic schemes and the same small letter with a subscript to denote its parts, e.g.
\mathbf{Z}=(Z_{F},(Z_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}}(\xi_{Z,v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
. We will also not mention the
\xi_{v}
when defining an adelic scheme if they are clear from the context.

An adelic scheme
\mathbf{X}
such that
X_{F}
and all the
X_{v}
are varieties will be called an
adelic variety
.

Example 3.3
.

Let
X
be a scheme over
F
. The corresponding
constant
adelic scheme is given by
(X,(X_{F_{v}})_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(\text{id}_{X_{F_{v}}})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
. We will freely identify a scheme with the corresponding constant adelic scheme.

For any adelic scheme
\mathbf{X}
there is a canonical map
X_{F}\to\mathbf{X}
of adelic schemes whose local components are given by the
\xi_{v}
.

Non-trivial examples of adelic varieties will be given in the following subsections.

Construction 3.4
.

Let
F^{\prime}/F
be a finite extension of number fields. Given an adelic variety
\mathbf{X}
over
F
we can construct an adelic variety
\mathbf{X}_{F^{\prime}}
over
F^{\prime}
as follows.

For every place
w\in\Omega_{F^{\prime}}
let
v\in\Omega_{F}
be the place over which it lies and consider the induced field extension
F^{\prime}_{w}/F_{v}
. Let
X_{F^{\prime}}:=(X_{F})_{F^{\prime}}
and
X_{w}:=(X_{v})_{F^{\prime}_{w}}
be the corresponding base changes. The map
\xi_{v}
then induces after base change a map
\xi_{w}:(X_{F^{\prime}})_{F^{\prime}_{w}}\to X_{w}
. We define
\mathbf{X}_{F^{\prime}}:=(X_{F^{\prime}},(X_{w})_{w\in\Omega_{F^{\prime}}},(\xi_{w})_{w\in\Omega_{F^{\prime}}})
.

We have a good notion of adelic points on adelic varieties.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be an adelic variety over
F
,
S
a finite set of places and
\mathcal{X}
an
\mathcal{O}_{F,S}
-integral model of
X_{F}
. The
space of adelic points of

\mathbf{T}
is the restricted product of topological spaces

\mathbf{X}(\mathbb{A}_{F}):=\prod_{v\in S}X_{v}(F_{v})\times\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\prod}_{v\not\in S}(X_{v}(F_{v}),\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{v})).

This is independent of the choice of integral model and
S
for the usual reason.

There is a diagonal map
X_{F}(F)\to\mathbf{X}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
. This map is an injection since
X_{F}(F)\subset X_{F}(F_{v})=X_{v}(F_{v})
for all but finitely many places
v
.

The following two cohomological invariants of adelic varieties will play a crucial role in what follows. Recall that the
(cohomological) Brauer group
of a scheme
X
is
\Br X:=\HH^{2}(X,\mathbb{G}_{m})
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be an adelic variety over
F
.

(1)

A
regular function
of
\mathbf{X}
is a map
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{f}
. Equivalently it is a tuple
(f_{v},(f_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
where
f_{F}\in\HH^{0}(X_{F},\mathcal{O}_{X_{F}})
and
f_{v}\in\HH^{0}(X_{v},\mathcal{O}_{X_{v}})
such that
\xi_{v}^{*}(f_{v})=f_{F}
. Let
\HH^{0}(\mathbf{X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}})
be the
F
-vector space of regular functions, where addition and scalar multiplication is done component-wise.

(2)

The
Picard group

\Pic\mathbf{X}
of
\mathbf{X}
is the group of tuples
(\mathcal{L}_{F},((\mathcal{L}_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
where
\mathcal{L}_{F}\in\Pic X_{F}
and
\mathcal{L}_{v}\in\Pic X_{v}
such that
\xi_{v}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{v}=(\mathcal{L}_{F})_{F_{v}}\in\Pic(X_{F})_{F_{v}}
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
. Addition is component-wise.

(3)

The
Brauer group

\Br\mathbf{X}
of
\mathbf{X}
is the group consisting of tuples
(A_{F},(A_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
where
A_{F}\in\Br X_{F}
and
A_{v}\in\Br X_{v}
such that
\xi_{v}^{*}A_{v}=(A)_{F_{v}}\in\Br(X_{F})_{F_{v}}
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
. Addition is component-wise.

The
constant Brauer group

\Br_{0}\mathbf{X}
is defined as the image of the natural map
\Br F\to\Br\mathbf{X}
.

The
algebraic Brauer group

\Br_{1}\mathbf{X}
is the subgroup of
\Br\mathbf{X}
consisting of all tuples
(A_{F},(A_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
such that
(A_{v})_{\overline{F}_{v}}=0\in\Br(X_{v})_{\overline{F}_{v}}
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
. This also implies that
(A_{F})_{\overline{F}}=0\in\Br(X_{F})_{\overline{F}}
.

We note that these definitions agree with the usual ones if we identify a variety with the corresponding adelic variety.

Definition 3.7
.

An
effective (Cartier) divisor

\mathbf{D}\subset\mathbf{X}
is an adelic subvariety such that
D_{F}\subset X_{F}
and
D_{v}\subset X_{v}
for all places
v\in\Omega_{F}
is an effective (Cartier) divisor and
\xi_{v}^{-1}(D_{v})=(D_{F})_{F_{v}}
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
.

Given an effective Cartier divisor
\mathbf{D}\subset\mathbf{X}
we define the corresponding adelic line bundle by
\mathcal{O}(\mathbf{D})=(\mathcal{O}(D),(\mathcal{O}(D_{v}))_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
.

Assuming that
\Pic\mathbf{X}
is finitely generated we let the
pseudo-effective cone

\Eff_{\mathbf{X}}\subset(\Pic\mathbf{X})_{\mathbb{R}}
be the closure of the cone generated by the effective Cartier divisors.

3.1.
Clemens complexes

The examples of adelic varieties we will consider in this paper will all be associated to faces of Clemens complexes. Let us first recall the definition from
[
CLT10a
, §3.1]

Definition 3.8
.

Let
X
be a variety over a field
F
and
D\subset X
a normal crossing divisor. Let
\mathcal{A}_{D}
be the set of irreducible components of
D
, if
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{D}
then we write
D_{\alpha}
for the corresponding irreducible component.

(1)

The
Clemens complex

\mathcal{C}(D)
is the poset which has as elements pairs
(A,Z)
with
A\subset\mathcal{A}_{D}
and
Z
an irreducible component of
\cap_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}D_{\alpha}
. We call such a pair a
face
. The order is defined by
(A,Z)\preceq(A^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
if
A\subset A^{\prime}
and
Z\supset Z^{\prime}
.

(2)

The
geometric Clemens complex

\mathcal{C}^{\text{geom}}(D)
is the Clemens complex
\mathcal{C}(D_{\overline{F}})
of
D_{\overline{F}}\subset X_{\overline{F}}
.

(3)

If
F
is a number field and
v
a place then we define the
v
-analytic Clemens complex

\mathcal{C}_{v}^{\text{an}}(D)
as the subset of those
(A,Z)\in\mathcal{C}^{\text{geom}}(D)
such that
Z
is defined over
F_{v}
and
Z(F_{v})\neq\emptyset
. The subset of maximal faces is denoted
\mathcal{C}_{v}^{\text{an,max}}(D)
.

(4)

If
S
is a set of places of
F
then the
S
-analytic Clemens complex
is
\mathcal{C}^{\text{an}}_{S}(D):=\prod_{v\in S}\mathcal{C}_{v}^{\text{an}}(D)
. The subset of maximal faces is denoted
\mathcal{C}_{S}^{\text{an,max}}(D)
.

Recall that the
dimension

\dim(p)
of an element
p\in P
of a finite poset
(P,\preceq)
is the maximal
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that there exists a chain
p_{0}\prec\cdots\prec p_{n}\prec p
. We will use the convention that the dimension of a minimal element is
-1
. As a Clemens complex is a poset its elements have a dimension.

The Galois group
\Gamma_{F}
acts on
\mathcal{C}^{\text{geom}}_{D}
in the obvious way. If
F
is a number field and
v
a place of
F
then
(A,Z)\in\mathcal{C}^{\text{geom}}(D)
lies in
\mathcal{C}^{\text{an}}_{v}(D)
if and only if it is fixed by
\Gamma_{v}
and
Z(F_{v})\neq\emptyset
.

We will write elements of
\mathcal{C}_{S}^{\text{an,max}}(D)
as
\mathbf{A}=((A_{v},Z_{v}))_{v}
and use the convention that
(A_{v},Z_{v}):=(\emptyset,X)
if
v\not\in S
.
We will associate the following spaces to faces of Clemens complexes.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
F
be a number field,
X
a variety over
F
and
D\subset X
a normal crossing divisor. Write
U:=X\setminus D
.

(1)

Let
v
be a place of
v
and
(A_{v},Z_{v})\in\mathcal{C}_{v}^{\text{an}}(D)
a face. The
interior
of
Z_{v}
is defined as

Z_{v}^{\circ}:=Z_{v}\setminus\bigcup_{(A_{v},Z_{v})\prec(A,Z)}Z.

(2)

Let
v
be a place of
v
and
(A_{v},Z_{v})\in\mathcal{C}_{v}^{\text{an}}(D)
a face. We define
the variety of
v
-adic points near
(A_{v},Z_{v})
as

(X;A_{v},Z_{v}):=X_{F_{v}}\setminus\bigcup_{(A,Z)\nprec(A_{v},Z_{v})}Z=U_{F_{v}}\cup\bigcup_{(A,Z)\preceq(A_{v},Z_{v})}Z^{\circ}.

(3)

Let
S
be a finite set of places and
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{C}^{\text{an}}_{S}(D)
a face. We define
the adelic variety of points near
\mathbf{A}
as the adelic variety

(X;{\mathbf{A}}):=(U,((X;A_{v},Z_{v}))_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(\xi_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})

where
\xi_{v}:U_{F_{v}}\to(X;A_{v},Z_{v})
is the inclusion and we used the convention that
(A_{v},Z_{v})=(\emptyset,X)
if
v\not\in S
.

The adelic variety
(X;{\mathbf{A}})
has the following space of adelic points

(X;{\mathbf{A}})(\mathbb{A}_{F})=U(\mathbb{A}_{F,S})\times\prod_{v\in S}(X;A_{v},Z_{v})(F_{v}).

Note that for all
v\in S
the inclusion
Z_{v}^{\circ}\subset(X;A_{v},Z_{v})
is a closed immersion. So
Z_{v}^{\circ}(F_{v})\subset(X;A_{v},Z_{v})(F_{v})
is a closed subset and taking the restricted product over all places we get a closed subset

U(\mathbb{A}_{F})_{\mathbf{A}}:=U(\mathbb{A}_{F,S})\times\prod_{v\in S}Z_{v}^{\circ}(F_{v})=\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\prod}_{v\in\Omega_{F}}Z_{v}^{\circ}(F_{v})\subset(X;{\mathbf{A}})(\mathbb{A}_{F}).

We can compute the cohomological invariants of
(X;\mathbf{A})
in terms of those of
X
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
X,D
and
\mathbf{A}
be as above and assume that
X
is smooth and
\overline{F}[U]^{\times}=\overline{F}^{\times}
.

The following sequence of finitely generated groups is exact

0\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{A}_{D}/\Gamma_{F}]\to\Pic X\times\prod_{v\in S}\mathbb{Z}[A_{v}/\Gamma_{v}]\to\Pic(X;\mathbf{A})\to 0

The first map sends
a\in\mathcal{A}_{D}/\Gamma
to
(\sum_{\alpha\in a}[D_{\alpha}],(\sum_{\alpha\Gamma_{v}\subset a\Gamma_{F}}\alpha\Gamma_{v})_{v\in S})
. The second map sends

L\in\Pic X
to
(L,(L_{v})_{v})\in\Pic(X;\mathbf{A})
and for
v\in S
it sends
a\Gamma_{v}\in A_{v}/\Gamma_{v}
to
(0,(0)_{w\neq v},[\sum_{\alpha\in a\Gamma_{v}}D_{\alpha}])
.
Moreover, if
\Pic X
is finitely generated then
\Eff_{(X;\mathbf{A})}
is the cone generated by the images of
\Eff_{X}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[A_{v}/\Gamma_{v}]
.

The Brauer group satisfies
\Br(X;\mathbf{A})\subset\Br U
and

\Br(X;\mathbf{A})=\{b\in\Br U:b_{F_{v}}\in\emph{im}(\Br(X;A_{v},Z_{v})\to\Br U)\emph{forall}v\in S\}.

Proof.

The first statement follows by combining that irreducible elements of
D
, resp.
D_{F_{v}}
, correspond to
\Gamma_{F}
-orbits, resp.
\Gamma_{v}
-orbits, of
\mathcal{A}_{D}
, Lemma
2.1
and the definition of
\Pic(X;\mathbf{A})
.

The second statement follows from the definition of
\Br(X;\mathbf{A})
and that
\Br(X;A_{v},Z_{v})\subset\Br U
for all
v
by Grothendieck purity
[
CTS21
, Thm. 3.7.1]
.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

It follows from the above lemma and
[
Wil22
, Lemma 2.2.3]
that in the split case and if
\overline{F}[U]^{\times}=\overline{F}^{\times}
then
\Pic(X;\mathbf{A})
agrees with what is denoted
\Pic(U;\mathbf{A})
by Wilsch.

Corollary 3.12
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
3.10
we have
\emph{rk}(\Pic(X;\mathbf{A}))=\emph{rk}(\Pic U)+\dim\mathbf{A}+1
.

Proof.

Note that
\text{rk}(\Pic X)=\text{rk}(\Pic U)+\#\mathcal{A}_{D}/\Gamma_{F}
and
\dim\mathbf{A}=\sum_{v\in S}\#A_{v}/\Gamma_{v}-1
by
[
CLT10a
, p. 380]
. This equality then follows from the lemma.
∎

It is known that integral points tend to concentrate around maximal faces of the analytic Clemens complex. It was observed by Wilsch
[
Wil22
, §2.3]
that for split varieties there can be analytic obstructions to integral points near certain faces of the Clemens complex. The argument easily generalizes to arbitrary adelic varieties.

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be an adelic variety with a non-constant regular function, i.e.
\HH^{0}(\mathbf{X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{X}})\neq F
. Then for any compact subset
C\subset\mathbf{X}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
the set
X_{F}(F)\cap C
is not Zariski-dense in
\mathbf{X}
in the sense that there exists a proper closed adelic subvariety
\mathbf{D}\subsetneq\mathbf{X}
such that
X_{F}(F)\cap C\subset D_{F}(F)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{f}:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
be a non-constant regular function. The map
\mathbf{f}:\mathbf{X}(\mathbb{A}_{F})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{F})=\mathbb{A}_{F}
is continuous so the image
\mathbf{f}(C)
is compact. The intersection of
\mathbf{f}(C)
with the discrete subset
\mathbb{A}^{1}(F)=F
is thus finite.

Let
\mathbf{D}:=\mathbf{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{f}(C)\cap F)
. We clearly have
X_{F}(F)\cap C\subset D_{F}(F)
and
\mathbf{D}\neq\mathbf{X}
because
\mathbf{f}
is non-constant.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

Our application of this theorem is the following. Let
X,D,\mathbf{A}
be as above. Let
\mathcal{X}
be a proper
\mathcal{O}_{F,S}
-integral model of
X
,
\mathcal{D}
the closure of
D
in
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{U}:=\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{D}
.

For each
v\in S
let
C_{v}\subset(X;A_{v},Z_{v})(F_{v})
be a compact subspace. We can then consider the compact subspace
\prod_{v\not\in S}\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{O}_{v})\times\prod_{v\in S}C_{v}\subset(X;\mathbf{A})(\mathbb{A}_{F})
. If
\HH^{0}((X;\mathbf{A}),\mathcal{O}_{(X;\mathbf{A})})\neq F
then Theorem
3.13
implies that the set of
\mathcal{O}_{F,S}
-integral points of
\mathcal{U}
which lie in
C_{v}
for all
v\in S
is not Zariski dense. This generalizes
[
Wil22
, Prop. 3.2.1]
.

This leads us to the following definition.

Definition 3.15
.

The face
\mathbf{A}
has an
analytic obstruction (to Zariski density of integral points)
if
\HH^{0}((X;\mathbf{A}),\mathcal{O}_{(X;\mathbf{A})})\neq F
.

We will apply Theorem
3.13
to deal with the faces of the Clemens complex where we cannot apply our method. The reason why our method fails in this case is that the conclusion of the following proposition will not hold.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
X
be a smooth proper variety over
F
such that
\Pic X_{\overline{F}}
is finitely generated. Let
D\subset X
be a geometrically simple normal crossing divisor,
S
a finite set of places and
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{C}^{\an}_{S}(D)
a face of the analytic Clemens complex.

Assume that the pseudo-effective cone
\Eff_{X}
is generated by the effective divisors.

If
\mathbf{A}
has an analytic obstruction then
\Eff_{(X;\mathbf{A})}\cap-\Eff_{(X;\mathbf{A})}=0
.

Proof.

This is a minor generalization of
[
Wil22
, Thm. 2.4.1]
and the same argument works.
∎

3.2.
Adelic groups of multiplicative type

An important class of adelic varieties are adelic groups of multiplicative type. We will also consider the corresponding adelic family of their character groups.

Definition 3.17
.

(1)

An
adelic group of multiplicative type over
F
is an adelic variety
\mathbf{T}=(T_{F},(T_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(\tau_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
where
T_{F}
, resp.
T_{v}
, is a group of multiplicative type over
F
, resp.
F_{v}
, and
\tau_{v}
is a group homomorphism
(T_{F})_{F_{v}}\to T_{v}
.

(2)

A homomorphism
\mathbf{f}:\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{T^{\prime}}
of adelic groups of multiplicative type is a morphism of adelic varieties such that all the
f_{v}
are homomorphisms of groups.

(3)

An
adelic module over
F
is a triple
\mathbf{L}=(L_{F},(L_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(\lambda_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
where
L_{F}
, resp.
L_{v}
, is abelian group with a continuous
\Gamma_{F}
, resp.
\Gamma_{v}
-action, and
\lambda_{v}
is a map
L_{v}\to L_{F}
of
\Gamma_{v}
-modules.

If
L_{F}
and all the
L_{v}
are finitely generated and
\lambda_{v}
is an isomorphism for all but finitely many
v
then we say that
\mathbf{L}
is finitely generated.

(4)

Let
\mathbf{L}=(L_{F},(L_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(\lambda_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
and
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}=(L^{\prime}_{F},(L^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(\lambda^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
be adelic modules over
F
. A map
\bm{f}:\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
is a tuple
(f_{F},(f_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
where
f_{F}:L_{F}\to L^{\prime}_{F}
is a morphism of
\Gamma_{F}
-modules and
f_{v}:L_{v}\to L^{\prime}_{v}
is a morphism of
\Gamma_{v}
modules such that
f_{F}\circ\lambda_{v}=\lambda^{\prime}_{v}\circ f_{v}
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
.

(5)

If
\mathbf{T}=(T_{F},(T_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(\tau_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
is an adelic group of multiplicative type then its
group of characters
is the finitely generated adelic module

X^{*}(\mathbf{T}):=(X^{*}(T_{F}),(X^{*}(T_{v}))_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(X^{*}(\tau_{v}))_{v\in\Omega_{F}}).

The group of
global characters
of
\mathbf{T}
is
\hat{\mathbf{T}}:=\Hom(\mathbf{T},\mathbb{G}_{m})\cong\Hom(\mathbb{Z},X^{*}(\mathbf{T}))
.

(6)

If
\mathbf{L}=(L_{F},(L_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(\lambda_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
is an finitely generated adelic module then its
Cartier dual
is the adelic group of multiplicative type

D(\mathbf{L}):=(D(L_{F}),(D(L_{v}))_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(D(\lambda_{v}))_{v\in\Omega_{F}}).

The functors
X^{*}(\cdot)
and
D(\cdot)
clearly define an contravariant equivalence between the categories of adelic groups of multiplicative type and the finitely generated adelic modules.

We can define the Galois cohomology of adelic modules and adelic groups of multiplicative type.

Definition 3.18
.

Let
\mathbf{L}
be an adelic module over
F
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We define the
i
-th Galois cohomology group
as the following group, where addition is component-wise.

\HH^{i}(F,\mathbf{L}):=\{(\alpha_{F},(\alpha_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}}):\alpha_{F}\in\HH^{i}(F,L_{F}),\alpha_{v}\in\HH^{i}(F_{v},L_{v})\text{ such that }\lambda_{v*}\alpha_{v}=(\alpha_{F})_{F_{v}}\}.

Let
S\subset\Omega_{F}
be a finite set of places such that
L_{v}
is unramified for
v\not\in S
. The
adelic Galois cohomology
of
\mathbf{L}
is the restricted product

\HH^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{F},\mathbf{L}):=\prod_{v\in S}\HH^{i}(F_{v},L_{v})\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\prod}_{v\in\Omega_{F}\setminus S}(\HH^{i}(F_{v},L_{v}),\HH^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{v},L_{v})).

The
Tate-Shafarevich group
of
\mathbf{L}
is

\Sha^{i}(F,\mathbf{L}):=\ker(\HH^{i}(F,\mathbf{L})\to\HH^{i}(\mathbb{A}_{F},\mathbf{L})).

Definition 3.19
.

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an adelic group of multiplicative type and
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Its
i
-th Galois cohomology group
is defined as
\HH^{i}(F,\mathbf{T}):=\HH^{i}(F,T_{F})
.

Let
S\subset\Omega_{F}
be a finite set of places such that
T_{v}
has good reduction when
v\not\in S
. We define the
adelic Galois cohomology
of
\mathbf{T}
as the restricted product

\HH^{i}(\mathbb{A}_{F},\mathbf{T}):=\prod_{v\in S}\HH^{i}(F_{v},T_{v})\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\prod}_{v\in\Omega_{F}\setminus S}(\HH^{i}(F_{v},T_{v}),\HH^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{v},T_{v})).

This is obviously independent of
S
.

The
Tate-Shafarevich group
of
\mathbf{T}
is

\Sha^{i}(F,\mathbf{T}):=\ker(\HH^{i}(F,\mathbf{T})\to\HH^{i}(\mathbb{A}_{F},\mathbf{T}))

Let us note the following simple fact.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
\mathbf{L}
be an adelic module. We have
\Hom(\mathbb{Z},\mathbf{L})=\HH^{0}(F,\mathbf{L})
.

Proof.

This follows after unfolding definitions.
∎

Remark 3.21
.

The category of adelic modules is abelian so one might expect the natural cohomology theory on it to be given by the Ext groups
\text{Ext}^{i}(\mathbb{Z},\cdot)
. It turns out that this is not isomorphic to the cohomology we defined. For example, if
\mathbf{L}:=(\mathbb{Z},(0)_{v\in\Omega_{F}})
then
\HH^{1}(F,\mathbf{L})=0
but
\text{Ext}^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbf{L})\cong(\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}}\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}
.

Tate-Shafarevich groups are finite for finitely generated adelic modules and groups of multiplicative type.

Lemma 3.22
.

(1)

Let
\mathbf{L}
be an adelic module over
F
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\Sha^{i}(F,\mathbf{L})=\Sha^{i}(F,L_{F})
. In particular,
\Sha^{i}(F,\mathbf{L})
is finite if
L_{F}
is finitely generated.

(2)

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an adelic group of multiplicative type. Then
\Sha^{1}(F,\mathbf{T})
is finite.

Proof.

For the first part let
(\alpha,(\alpha_{v})_{v\in\Omega_{F}})\in\Sha^{i}(F,\mathbf{L}))
. Then
\alpha_{v}=0
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
by the definition of
\Sha^{i}(F,\mathbf{L})
. By the definition of
\HH^{i}(F,\mathbf{L})
this implies that
(\alpha)_{F_{v}}=0
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
, i.e.
\alpha\in\Sha^{i}(F,L_{F})
. Conversely, if
\alpha\in\Sha^{i}(F,L_{F})
then
(\alpha,(0)_{v\in\Omega_{F}})\in\Sha^{i}(F,\mathbf{L}))
. Finiteness follows from
[
Mil06
, Thm. 4.20]
.

For the second part let
S
is a finite set of places such that for all
v\not\in S

T_{v}=(T_{F})_{F_{v}}
. By definition we know that there exists an exact sequence

0\to\Sha^{1}(F,T_{F})\to\Sha^{1}(F,\mathbf{T})\to\oplus_{v\in S}\HH^{1}(F_{v},T_{F})

The lemma follows since
\Sha^{1}(F,T_{F})
and
\HH^{1}(F_{v},T_{F})
are finite for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
by
[
NSW08
, Thm. 8.6.7]
and
[
Mil06
, Cor. 2.3]
.
∎

The main examples of adelic modules appearing in this paper are adelic Picard groups.

Definition 3.23
.

The
adelic Picard group
of an adelic variety
\mathbf{X}
is the adelic module

\bm{\Pic}\mathbf{X}:=(\Pic X_{F,\overline{F}},(\Pic X_{v,\overline{F}_{v}})_{v\in\Omega_{F}},(\xi_{v}^{*})_{v\in\Omega_{F}}).

There is an obvious map of adelic modules
\Pic\mathbf{X}\to\bm{\Pic}\mathbf{X}
.

Lemma 3.24
.

If
\mathbf{X}
is an adelic variety such that
X_{F}(F)\neq\emptyset
then
\Pic\mathbf{X}\cong\Hom(\mathbb{Z},\bm{\Pic}\mathbf{X})
.

Proof.

If
X_{F}(F)\neq\emptyset
then also
X_{v}(F_{v})\neq 0
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
. These facts imply that
\Pic X_{F}=(\Pic X_{F,\overline{F}})^{\Gamma_{F}}
and
\Pic X_{v}\cong(\Pic X_{v,\overline{F}_{v}})^{\Gamma_{v}}
. The lemma then follows from the definition.
∎

Moreover,
\bm{\Pic}\mathbf{X}
is also related to the algebraic Brauer group
\Br_{1}\mathbf{X}
. Indeed, by the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence there are maps
r:\Br_{1}X_{F}\to\HH^{1}(F,\Pic X_{F})
and
r_{v}:\Br_{1}X_{v}\to\HH^{1}(F_{v},\Pic X_{v})
. By the functoriality of the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence these fit together into a map
r:\Br_{1}\mathbf{X}\to\HH^{1}(F,\bm{\Pic}\mathbf{X})
.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be an adelic variety such that
\overline{F}[X_{F}]^{\times}=\overline{F}^{\times}
and
\overline{F}_{v}[X_{v}]^{\times}=\overline{F}_{v}^{\times}
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
. The map
r:\Br_{1}\mathbf{X}/\Br_{0}\mathbf{X}\to\HH^{1}(F,\bm{\Pic}\mathbf{X})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Under the assumptions of the lemma the maps
r:\Br_{1}X_{F}/\Br_{0}X_{F}\to\HH^{1}(F,\Pic X_{F})
and
r_{v}:\Br_{1}X_{v}/\Br_{0}X_{v}\to\HH^{1}(F_{v},\Pic X_{v})
are all isomorphisms
[
Sko01
, Cor. 2.3.9]
. The lemma follows.
∎

Note that the topological space
\mathbf{T}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
is a topological group because it is the restricted product of topological groups. We can generalize the norm and the notion of norm
1
elements to adelic groups of multiplicative type.

Definition 3.26
.

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an adelic group of multiplicative type over
F
.
We define the
norm map
as the map

|\cdot|_{\mathbf{T}}:\mathbf{T}(\mathbb{A}_{F})\to\Hom(\hat{\mathbf{T}},\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\times}):(t_{v})_{v}\to\left((\chi,(\chi_{v})_{v})\to\prod_{v}|\chi_{v}(t_{v})|_{v}\right).

We will call its kernel the
group of norm
1
elements
and denote it by
\mathbf{T}(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{1}
.

We note that
T_{F}(F)\subset\mathbf{T}(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{1}
by the product formula.

Lemma 3.27
.

The norm map is surjective if
T_{F}\to T_{v}
is an isomorphism for all non-archimedean
v
.

Proof.

We will show that the log norm is surjective.

Consider the following commutative diagram

(5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{F}(\mathbb{A}_{F})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{T}(\mathbb{A}_{F})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{v}\coker(T_{F}(F_{v})\to T_{v}(F_{v}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(L_{F,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{F}})^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{v}\coker((L_{F,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}})^{*}\to(L_{v,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}})^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\log|\cdot|_{\mathbf{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\log|\cdot|_{T_{F}}}

The first row is exact by definition and the second row is exact since it is the dual of the following sequence tensored by
\mathbb{R}
.

0\to\bigoplus_{v}\ker(L_{v}^{\Gamma_{v}}\to L_{F}^{\Gamma_{v}})\to\hat{\mathbf{T}}\to\hat{T}_{F}

This sequence is exact by the definition of
\hat{\mathbf{T}}
.

The map
\log|\cdot|_{T_{F}}
is surjective so by the four lemma it suffices to show that
\prod_{v}\coker(T_{F}(F_{v})\to T_{v}(F_{v}))\to\prod_{v}\coker((L_{F,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}})^{*}\to(L_{v,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}})^{*})
is surjective. This follows from the fact that for each
v\in\Omega_{F}^{\infty}
the map
\log|\cdot|_{T_{v}}:T_{v}(F_{v})\to(L_{v,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}})^{*}
is surjective.
∎

Lemma 3.28
.

If
(T_{F})_{F_{v}}\to T_{v}
is a closed immersion for all
v
then
T(F)\subset\mathbf{T}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
is a discrete inclusion and
\mathbf{T}(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{1}/T_{F}(F)
is compact.

Proof.

In this case
T_{F}(\mathbb{A}_{F})\subset\mathbf{T}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
is a closed inclusion and
T_{F}(F)\subset T_{F}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
is a discrete inclusion.

It follows from the condition that
L_{v,\mathbb{R}}\to L_{F,\mathbb{R}}
is surjective. This implies that
L_{v,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}}\to L_{F,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}}
is surjective because
\Gamma_{v}
is compact. It follows from the definition of
\hat{\mathbf{T}}
that
\hat{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbb{R}}\to L_{F,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{F}}
is surjective.

Applying this and the diagram (
5
) we deduce that the following sequence is exact.

T_{F}(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{1}\to\mathbf{T}(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{1}\to\prod_{v}\ker\left(T_{v}(F_{v})/T_{F}(F_{v})\to(L_{v,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}})^{*}/(L_{F,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}})^{*}\right).

We have
X^{*}(T_{v}/T_{F})=\ker(L_{v}\to L_{F})
. The group
T_{F}(F_{v})/T_{v}(F_{v})
injects into
T_{F}/T_{v}(F_{v})
and the map
T_{v}(F_{v})/T_{F}(F_{v})\to(L_{v,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}})^{*}/(L_{F,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}})^{*}
is the composition of this injection and
\log|\cdot|_{T_{v}/T_{F}}:T_{v}/T_{F}(F_{v})\to X^{*}(T_{v}/T_{F})_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}=(L_{v,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}})^{*}/(L_{F,\mathbb{R}}^{\Gamma_{v}})^{*}
by functoriality. The kernel of this map is thus compact for all
v
.

Since
T_{F}(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{1}/T_{F}(F)
is also compact this implies that
\mathbf{T}(\mathbb{A}_{F})^{1}/T_{F}(F)
is compact.
∎

To relate the Brauer-Manin obstruction to the descent obstruction for adelic varieties we will need a form of Poitou-Tate duality for adelic groups of multiplicative type, generalizing that of groups of multiplicative type
[
Mil06
]
.

3.2.1.
Local Poitou-Tate duality

Let
F
be a local field of characteristic
p
,
T
a smooth
2
2

2

Equivalently,
L
has no
p
-torsion.
group of multiplicative type with
\Gamma_{F}
module of characters
L
. Recall from class field theory that there is an injective invariant map
\text{inv}_{F}:\HH^{2}(F,\mathbb{G}_{m})=\Br(F)\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
. The bilinear pairing
T\times L\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
then induces a perfect pairing
[
Mil06
, Cor. 2.3,Thm. 2.13]

(6)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\text{PT},F}:\HH^{1}(F,T)\times\HH^{1}(F,L)\to H^{2}(F,\mathbb{G}_{m})\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}:(a_{v},b_{v})\to\text{inv}_{F}(a_{v}\cup b_{v}).

Moreover, if
T
has good reduction over
\mathcal{O}_{F}
then the orthogonal complement of
\HH^{1}(\mathcal{O},T)\subset\HH^{1}(F,T)
with respect to this pairing is
\HH^{1}(\mathcal{O},L)\subset\HH^{1}(F,T)

[
Mil06
, Thm. 2.6]
.

The Poitou-Tate pairing is functorial since the cup product is. To be precise, let
\tau:T\to T^{\prime}
be a morphism of groups of multiplicative type and
\lambda:L^{\prime}\to L
the dual morphism of their
\Gamma_{F}
-modules of characters. Then for all
a\in\HH^{1}(F,T)
and
b\in\HH^{1}(F,L)
we have the equality

(7)

\langle a,\lambda_{*}b\rangle_{\text{PT},F}=\langle\tau_{*}a,b\rangle_{\text{PT},F}.

3.2.2.
Global Poitou-Tate duality

Let
F
be a number field,
\mathbf{T}
an adelic group of multiplicative type with adelic module of characters
\mathbf{L}
.

For each place
v
of
F
we get a local Poitou-Tate pairing (
6
)
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\text{PT},F_{v}}
. The group of multiplicative type
T_{v}
has good reduction at all but finitely many places. By the orthogonality of
\HH^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{v},T_{v})
and
\HH^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{v},L_{v})
the restricted product of these pairings exists. It is explicitly given by the formula

(8)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\text{PT}}:\HH^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{F},\mathbf{T})\times\HH^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{F},\mathbf{L})\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}:((a_{v})_{v},(b_{v})_{v})\to\sum_{v\in\Omega_{F}}\text{inv}_{v}(a_{v}\cup b_{v}).

This pairing is perfect since all the local pairings are. We will need the following version of Poitou-Tate duality.

Proposition 3.29
.

The subgroup orthogonal the image of
\HH^{1}(F,T_{F})\to\HH^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{F},\mathbf{T})
with respect to the pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\emph{PT}}
is the image of
\HH^{1}(F,\mathbf{L})\to\HH^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{F},\mathbf{L})
.

Proof.

Let
(b_{v})_{v}\in\HH^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{F},\mathbf{L})
and
a\in\HH^{1}(F,T_{F})
. The maps
\tau_{v}:T\to T_{v}
and
\lambda_{v}:L_{v}\to L_{F}
are dual so by functoriality of the local Poitou-Tate pairing (
7
) we have

\langle a,(b_{v})_{v}\rangle_{\text{PT}}=\sum_{v\in\Omega_{F}}\text{inv}_{v}(\tau_{v}(a)\cup b_{v})=\sum_{v\in\Omega_{F}}\text{inv}_{v}(a\cup\lambda_{v}(b_{v}))=\langle a,(\lambda_{v}(b_{v}))_{v}\rangle_{\text{PT}}.

It follows from global Poitou-Tate duality
[
Mil06
, Thm. 4.20]
that
\langle a,(\lambda_{v}(b_{v}))_{v}\rangle_{\text{PT}}=0
for all
a\in\HH^{1}(F,T_{F})
if and only if there exists a
b\in\HH^{1}(F,L_{F})
such that
\lambda_{v}(b_{v})=b
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
. This is exactly what we had to prove.
∎

Remark 3.30
.

Poitou-Tate duality also includes a duality between
\Sha^{1}(F,T_{F})
and
\Sha^{2}(F,L_{F})
. There is no such duality between
\Sha^{1}(F,\mathbf{T})
and
\Sha^{2}(F,\mathbf{L})
in general. For example: if
F=\mathbb{Q}
,
L_{F}=L_{v}=\mathbb{Z}/8\mathbb{Z}
for all places
v\neq 2
and
L_{2}=0
then
\Sha^{1}(F,\mathbf{T})=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, the non-trivial class given by
16\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times 8}=\HH^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\mu_{8})
, but
\Sha^{2}(F,\mathbf{L})=\Sha^{2}(F,L_{F})=0
.

3.2.3.
Haar measures

In this section we will consider certain Haar measures related to adelic groups of multiplicative type.

We first discuss the local case. Let
F
be a local field and
T
be a smooth group of multiplicative type over
F
. Let
L:=X^{*}(L_{F^{\sep}})
be its
\Gamma_{F}
-module of characters.

Lemma 3.31
.

Let
\pi_{0}(T)
be the group scheme of connected components of
T
, equivalently the Cartier dual of the submodule
L_{\emph{tors}}\subset L
of torsion elements. If
T
has good reduction then we have
\#\HH^{1}(\mathcal{O},T)=\#\pi_{0}(T)(F)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be the residue field of
F
and
I:=\ker(\Gamma_{F}\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}})
the inertia group. Because
L
is finitely generated and
\mathcal{O}
is henselian we have
\HH^{1}(\mathcal{O},L)=\HH^{1}(\mathbb{F},L)
. The exact sequence
1\to I\to\Gamma_{F}\to\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}\to 1
splits since
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}\cong\hat{\mathbb{Z}}
is free. The Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence
\HH^{p}(I,\HH^{q}(\mathbb{F},L))\implies\HH^{p+q}(F,L)
thus induces a split exact sequence

0\to\HH^{1}(\mathbb{F},L)\to\HH^{1}(F,L)\to\HH^{1}(I,L)^{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}}\to 0.

We find that
\#\HH^{1}(\mathcal{O},T)=\#\HH^{1}(I,L)^{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}}
since
\HH^{1}(\mathbb{F},L)
is the orthogonal complement of
\HH^{1}(\mathcal{O},T)
. The action of
I
on
L
is trivial so
\HH^{1}(I,L)=\Hom(I,L)
. The
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}
-module
I
is isomorphic to the Tate twist
\hat{\mathbb{Z}}(1)
modulo
p
-torsion so since
L
has no
p
-torsion because
T
is smooth we deduce that
\Hom(I,L)\cong\Hom(\hat{\mathbb{Z}}(1),L)\cong L_{\text{tors}}(-1)
.

Consider the dual group
((L_{\text{tors}}(-1))^{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}})^{\vee}\cong(L_{\text{tors}}^{\vee}(1))_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}}=\pi_{0}(T)_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}}
where
(\cdot)_{\Gamma_{F}}
denotes the
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}
-coinvariants. A finite group has the same size as its dual so it remains to prove that
\#\pi_{0}(T)_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}}=\#\pi_{0}(T)(F)
. If
\sigma
denotes a topological generator of
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}\cong\hat{\mathbb{Z}}
